This is the directors’ report for The Co-operative Bank pic (Bank) It explains in detail how

the Bank has performed dunng 2012 and sets out a fair review of the business, a balanced and
comprehensive analysis of performance, the use of financial and non financial key performance
indicators to explain the progress made, a description of the principal nsks and uncertamnties faced
by the business and an indication of likely future developments

in prepanng the report, the Bank has had regard to the guidance issued by the Financial Reporting
Council (formerly the Accounting Standards Board} in its Reporting Statement on narratve reporting
It ts intended to provide Shareholders of the Bank and members of the Co-operative Group with a
greater understanding of the Bank, its position in the market and its prospects,

In setting out the Bank’s main nsks and uncertainties, and likely future developments, this report and
accounts contains statements which, by their nature, cannot be considered indcations of likelihood
or certainty The statements are based on the knowledge and information avaliable at the date of
preparation of the directors’ report, and what are believed to be reasonable judgments, and these
statements should not be construed as a profit forecast

About us: a guide to the Co-operative Bank

Who we are

The Co-operative Bank plc is part of the Co-operative
Banking Group, providing a range of financial products
and services to personal and business banking customers
across the UK.

The Co-operative Bank plc currently operates under its own
brand as well as those of smile, Platform and Britannia.
We offer our services through 339 branches and

22 corporate banking centres anc via telephone,
online and mobile banking.

We are a leader in the field of ethical investment

and corporate social responsibility, Our customer driven ethical
strategy was the first of its kind in our industry and we pursue
an active strategy of community involvement.

Banking Group) and its ultimate parent organisation is the Co-operative Group

Risk management

The Board and executive management have primary responsibilty for
Identifying the key business risks facing the Bank and for approving the
Bank's risk strategy and level of acceptable risk which is articulated through
tts statement of nsk appetite The risk management framework, which 6
approved and reviewed by the Bank Board, outlines the approach taken to
ensure a robust risk management process is in place throughout the Bank.

Dunng 2012 the Banking Group undertook a wide ranging review of rts

risk management framework which resulted in significant change in its
organisational design. The work on the risk management framework to date
has nicluded a redesign of the way the Bank classifies the risks tt faces,

the way nsk appetite is established and the structure and application of the
authority delegated by the Board to manage the risk within the agreed appetite

The Board accepts that there are nisks which could impact on the achievement
of the Bank's business objectives, but endeavours through positive risk
Tranagement sirategtes as outtined In the risk management framework, to
manage these ma manner that opumises returns within the confines of the
business’ risk appetite, whist protecting members’ interests and reserves

Further information on the Bank's approach to risk management Is given on
page 54 and details of KPls are on page 2

internal contro!

Intemal controls are the activites undertaken by management, the Board and
other parties to enhance risk management and Increase the probability that
established objectives and goals wil be acheved The Board has overall
responsibility for the Bank's system of intemal controls which aim to ensure
effective and efficent operations, qualrty of internal and external reporting,
safeguarding of the Bank's assets and compllarice with laws and regulations
The Bank operates a three lines of defence governance model to ensure
appropriate responsibility and accountability are maintained whitst recognising
that the system is designed to manage rather than eliminate rsk of failure to
achieve business objectives, It can only provide reasonable and not absolute
aSSUranoe against Maternal misstatement or logs.

The Bank's intemal control framework is designed to create an atihude of
talung acceptable business nsk within clearly defined limits which mcludes

* an organisational govemanice structure with clear ines of responsibility,
delegation of authority and reporting requirements,

checks and balances to ensure that business procasses operate
effmentiy reducing the nsk posed to the organisation whist adhenng
to organisational values,

* comprehensive systems of financial reporting The annual budget and long
term plan of the Bank and of each division are reviewed and approved by
the Board,

 acode of business conduct covering relations with customers, shareholders,
colleagues, suppliers, community and competitors,

* a whistleblowng policy and procedure which provides for any employee to
teport, in confidence, suspected serious malpractice,

© mtemal audit, nsk and compliance functions that review the system of
intemal control; and

* acontrol self assessment process designed to fully support the annual
review of the effectiveness of the system of intemal controf and enable
continual monitoring and improvement of the internal control environment.

The committee's responsibility Is to determine and oversee remuneration policy
that seeks to retam and motvate talented individuals, align with co-operabve
values and principles, and comply with best practice and FSA Remuneration
Code requrements

The committee reviews the ongoing appropriateness and relevance of
Temuneration policy and takes into account many factors including the need
for remuneration to be structured so as to link rewards to business and
individual performance

With regard to future remuneration policy, the committee beleves annual

and tong term incentive schemes to be appropriate and as such wil motwate
the senior executives to achieve short to medium and long term business
objectives

Ex post risk adjustment; the cancellation or reduction of varlable remuneration
through malus (for example clawback of an award for an executve action which
leads to customer detnment) wil be a major area of focus for the committee as
we review our future remuneration policies and practices.

In our report, we give further detalls on our remuneration policy and an
overview of senior executive remuneration for 2012

The Co-operative Group Remuneration and Appointments Committee governs

remuneration in respect of the role of the Bank's Chief Executive

Policy on senior executive remuneration
In determining the remuneration policy for senior executives, the committee
has considered a number of factors including

* the importance of attractng, retaining and motvabng senior executives
of the appropnate calibre io furthes the success of the Bank,

© the linking of reward to business and Individuat performance and the
application of co-operative values, which include a strong belief in
responsible stewardship of afl the Bank's resources and prudent
management of ask,

® the alignment of the interests of senior executives with those of the wider
Group and its members,

* pay prachces and conditions of all employees throughout the Bank,

» the motvational impact of moentive arrangements to ensure that they are
efiechve and do not adversely impact matters such as governance,
ermronmental and socal issues,

* 1. conjunction with the Group Remuneration and Appointments Committee
ensunng that pay practices are coherent with thosein the Co-operative
Group as a whole, and

* ensuring appropnale comphance with the FSA Remuneration Code

“romasuenensse | OL

The current policy is to pay senior executves base salaries around the market
Median, when compared wath other organisations of comparable size and
complexity in relevant business sectors The committee supports the pineiple
of performance related pay and operates an annual incentive plan (AIP) and a
long term incentive plan (TIP) which, together, mean that over 50% of the
remuneration package is performance related The committee does not
consider it appropnate to adhere to the sze of vanable pay typically available
In PLCs and accordmaty the amounts payable under these plans are lower
than in comparable PLCs Together, the AIP and LTIP represent total vanable
semuneration, with awards under the LTIP comprising the deferred element
of variable remuneration as required by the FSA

The committee considers that a successful remuneration policy needs to be
sufficiently flexible to take account of future changes in the Bank's business
environment, and m market remuneraton practice Accordingly, the commuttee
keeps the Bank's remuneration policy under review In particular, the absence
of highly ‘geared’ incentive plans (schemes which pay out signdicant multiples
of base salary when compared with some pracbces in the PLC sector} is
consistent with prudent risk management.

‘The commitiee notes that the rernuneraton practices already adopted by the
Bank, In ariditen to being good business practice, have heen endorsed by the
FSA through its Remuneration Code However, the committee will continue to
strive for further provements m its executive remunerabon arrangernents
as best practice evolves

Service contracts and exit payment policy

Itis the Bank's policy that the nobce period in sentor executives’ service
contracts should not exceed ane year All senlor executves have contracts

introduction

inflation being allowed to nse to improve growth tn response, a broad-ranging

Capital

The Group's policy 1s to conserve a robust capital base so as to maintain
investor, creditor and market confidence and to sustain future development
of the business However, the Group still recognises the need to mantain a
balance between the potential higher returns that might be achieved with
greater gearing, and the advantages and secunty afforded by a sound
capital position

Total capital resources are £2,578 2m (31 December 2011 £2,975 9m), with
Core Tier 1 capital after regulatory deductions of £1,5/6 8m (31 December
2011 £1,947 4m}

The Group’s capital position remams acceptable with a penod end Core Tier 1
positon of 8 8% 31 December 2011 9.6%) Throughout 2012, the Bank and
Its indivdually regulated operations have complied with all externally imposed
capital requirements However, the Board recogreses the need to build the
Capitalisation of the Group to provide increased resilience and capacity for
future growth Actions taken early in 2013 have already improved thes positon
192%

Current forecasts show that the Group's capital will remain above minimum
regulatory requirements over the penod of the Pan However, in response to
the impact of new Basel lit regulatons and the expectation of a prolonged
economic downtum, we are reviewing our business with the intent of improving
our profitability and capital positon Without management action, explained in
the Opportunities section below, compliance with regulatory capital
requirements would come under pressure

Liquidity
Group liquidity has been reviewed by considenng the latest liquidity forecast
(part of the Plan), as well as the stress testing results from 31 December 2012

of the intemal assessment of liquidity requirements, together with the Group's
compliance with its regulatory required levels

Ag at 31 December 2012, the Group held significant levels of surplus assets
above regulatory required levels, and had a liquid asset ratio of 14 6%
compared to 30 June 2012 of 12 7% and 31 December 2011 of 15 5%

The Group 1s predominantly customer focused and there are no significant
wholesale maturities due in 2013 Customer deposits have been very stable
through the downgrades that the Bank has previously expenenced Stress
testing forecasts, as at 31 December 2012, shows that sufficient liquidity
levels can be mamntained under the most severe scenarios Ths s also
documented mn the Individual Liquidity Adequacy Assessment (ILAA) The Bank
has avallable additional collateral equivalent to 20% of minimum regulatory
Tequirements which can be used in any of the Bank of England schemes

Further impairment of intangible assets, either due to finalisation of Project
Verde (purchase of {_loyds Banking Group branches) or, if Verde were not to
proceed, the decision to take a different strategic route in regard to banking
technology platforms The carrying value of the intanglble asset, in the books
of Co-operative Financial Services Management Services Limited, is £179m

© Further significant loan impairment, for example on higher nsk non-core
corporate lending The amount on the watchist not covered by collateral ts
£275m The collective impairment methodology takes into account the value
and risk relating to all exposures that are not already in default. There Is an
additonal nsk of collateral value erosion, mitigated by non-core corporate
disposals

© Conduct nsk issues requiring redress The costs of redress associated with
PPlare estimated at £244m Another industry-wide issue of this types a
nisk to the profitabiity of the Group The Group has no matenal exposure to

© Potential rating agency downgrades Although there is a nsk of downgrade

Opportunities
The Co-operative Bank brand continues to show resilience in these tough

evidenced by our strong customer advocacy scores and numbers of

The Board sponsored strategic review seeks to bud on this platform This
wide-ranging strategec review of our Banking Group businesses, considers the
management actions at the Board's disposal which will be undertaken in order
to improve profitability and capitalsabon of the Group mn ime with market
expectations and Basa lil capital requirements Initial action from this renew
was concluded in January 2013 when a secuntisation transaction was
completed to reduce the credit nsk on the balance sheet. This mproved the
Core Ter 1 raho by 0 4% The signing of the Sale and Purchase Agreement
and subsequent completion of the safe of our Life and Savings business,
followed by the planned downstreaming of capital into the Group will also have
positive implications for our capital position, subject to regulatory approval

Amongst other actions, we intend to significantly defeverage or reduce risk in
the balance sheet (faciitated to a large extent by the additional impairment
charges taken in 2012) and build upon the strength of the core business
through a simplification agenda and cost efficiency programme, as well as,
conservative credit nsk appetite and long term growth strategy focused on our
Gore relationship banking franchise Some of the actions under consideration,
for example, options around the sale of other Banking Group businesses (eg
General Insurance and Life and Savings businesses), will require support from
the immediate parent company, Co-operative Banking Group Limited, in order
to downstream capital nto the Group .

Statement of directors’ responsibilities in respect
of the report of the Board of Directors and the
annual report and financial statements

‘The directors are responsible for prepanng the annual report and the Group
and parent company financial statements in accordance with applicable law
and regulations

Company law requires the directors to prepare the financial statements for
each financial year Under that law they are required to prepare the financial
statements in accordance with internationat Financial Reporting Standards
(IFRS) as adopted by the EL! and applicable law and have elected to prepare
the parent company financial statements on the same basis

Under company law the directors must not approve the financial statements
unless they are satisfied that they gre a true and fair view of the state of affairs
of the Group and parent company and of their profit or loss for that peniod In
prepanng the financial statements, the directors are required to

select suitable accounting policies and then apply thern consistently,

© make judgments and estimates that are reasonable and prudent,

© state whether they have been prepared in accordance with IFRS as
adopted by the EU, and

© prepare the financial statements on the going concern basis unless
tis Inappropriate to presume that the Group and parent company will
continue in business

The directors are responsible for keeping adequate accounting records that
are sufficient to show and explain the Group’s transactions and disclose with
reasonable accuracy at any time the financial position of the Group and enable
them to ensure that its financial statements comply with the Companies Act
2006 They have general responsibility for taking such steps as are reasonably
open to them to safeguard the assets of the Group and to prevent and detect
fraud and other irregulanties

The directors have decided to prepare voluntarily a directors’ remuneration
report in accordance with Schedule 8 to The Large and Medium-sized
Companies and Groups (Accounts and Reports) Regulations 2008 made under
the Companies Act 2006, as if those requirements were to apply to the Bank
The directors have also decided to prepare voluntanty a corporate governance
statement as if the Bank were required to comply with the Listing Rules and the
Disclosure and Transparency Rules of the Financial Senaces Authonty in
relation to those matters

The directors are responsible for the maintenance and integnty of the corporate
and financial information included on the Bank's website Legislation in the UK
governing the preparation and dissemination of financial statements may differ
from legislation in other junsdicons

So far as the directors are aware, there is no relevant audit information of which
the Bank's auditors are unaware, and the directors have taken all steps that

they ought to have taken as directors in order to make themselves aware of any
relevant audit information and to establish that the Bank's auditors are aware of

that information Nh

Independent auditor’s report to the members of

The Co-operative Bank pic

We have audited the financial statements of The Co-operative Bank pic for the
year ended 31 December 2012 set out on pages 33 to 78, 80 and 81 and
pages 83 to 132 The financial reporting framework that has been applied in
their preparation 1s applicable law and Internationa! Financial Reporting
Standards (IFRSs) as adopted by the EU and, as regards the parent company
financial statements, as applied in accordance with the provisions of the
Companies Act 2006

This report is made solely to the company's members, as a body, in
accordance with Chapter 3 of Part 16 of the Companies Act 2006 and in
respect of reporting on corporate governance, on terms that have been agreed
Our audit work has been undertaken so that we might state to the company's
members those matters we are required to state to them in an auditor's report
and in respect of reporting on corporate governance, those matters that we
have agreed to state to them in our report, and for no other purpose To the
fullest extent permitted by law, we do not accept or assume responsibility to
anyone other than the company and the company’s members, as a body, for
our audit work, for this report, or for the opinions we have formed

Respective responsibilities of directors
and auditor

As explained more fully in the Directors’ Responsibilities Statement set out
on page 31, the directors are responsible for the preparation of the financial
statements and for being satisfied that they give a true and fair view Our
responsibility 1s to audit, and express an opinion on, the financial statements
in accordance with applicable law and Intemational Standards on Auditing
(UK and Ireland) Those standards require us to comply with the Auditing
Practices Board's Ethical Standards for Auditors

Basis of preparation

Both the parent company financial statements and the Group financial statements have been prepared and approved by the directors in accordance with
International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS) as issued by the internatonal Accounting Standards Board (ASB), and IFRS interpretations Committee (IFRIC)
guidance as adopted by the European Union (EU) On including the parent company financial statements bere together with the Group financial statements, the
Company ts taking advantage of the exemption in Section 408 of the Companies Act 2006 not to present ts indradual income statement and related notes that
form a part of these approved financial statements Information in respect of the Company alone is labelled throughout as ‘Bank’

The financial information has been prepared under the historic cost convention as modified by the revaluation of avatable for sale financial assets, derwvatiwe
contracts, investment properties and certam other financial assets and france liabilities held at far value The Group applies the recognition, measurement and
disclosure requirements of IFRS in issue that are endorsed by the EU and are effective for accountng petods beginning on or after 1 January 2012

Standards and interpretavons issued and effective
4 preparing these consolidated financial statements, the Group has adopted the following pronouncements during the year that are new or revised

* Amended IFRS 7 (Disclosures: Transfers of Financial Assets (2010)
This amendment’s otyectve 1s to promote the transparency of off balance sheet actiites, particularly n relation to the securtisation of fmancal assets
Specifically, the transference of these assets and their associated nsks are reported n @ way that ensures that the user of the accounts 1s able to understand the
fmancial position of such nsks being transferred The additianal disclosure 1s set out in note 39

* Amended IAS 72 (income Taxes Deferred Tax Recovery of Underlying Assets (2010)
This amendment’s objective 1s to refine JAS 12's measurement of deferred tax which relates to the recovery of an entity's assets Previously, the standard stated
that an asset's recovery would occur only when it was ether used or sald However, and as a consequence of this amendment, investment properties measured
at fair value are excluded from the standard's scope on recovery The amendment to IAS 12 has no matenal ampact on the finannal statements of the Group

Standards and interpretations issued but not yet effective

» IFRS 9 (Financal instruments Classification and Measurement (2010),
This new standard replaces IAS 39 (Financial Instruments Recognition and Measurement (2009)) Phase one requires financial assets to be classified as at
amortised cost or at fair value Further phases of IFRS 9 are scheduled to cover impairment and hedge accountng

Itbecomes effectve for accounting perlods beginning on or after 1 January 2015 Early adophon is permitted, once endorsed by the EU The impact of FAS 9 is
Jkely to be matenal to the Group, due to the value of financial instruments held on the Group's balance het The standard is currently being drafted and soa
detailed assessment of its impactis not currently possible

» IFRS 10 (Consolidated Financral Stataments (2011))
This new standard establishes principles for the presentahon and preparation of consolidated financial statements when an entity controls one or more other
entites Itsupersedes both IAS 27 (Consolidated and Separate Financial Statements (2008) except where JAS 27 stil applies as noted below, and Amended
SIC 12 (Consolidation Special Purpose Entities (2004) It aims to provide transparency in identifying off balance sheet, parent subsidiary relationships, using a
consistent basts for determining the existence and thus consolidation of those underlying entiyas being controlled by the reporting entity

Itbecomes effective for accounting penods beginning on or after 1 January 2013 It has been adopted for use within the EU for aecountng penods beginning on
orafter 1 January 2014 The impact to the Group of IFRS 10 fs tkely to be immaterial

* IFRS 11 (Joint Arrangements (2011)}
This new standard establishes principles far financial reporting by parties to a jont arrangement It supersedes both [AS 31 (Interests in dosnt Ventures (2010)
and Amended SIC 13 WJomtly Controlled Entihes Non-Monetary Contnbuttoris by Venturers (2007)) Recognition entena now distinguish between joint
operations and joint ventures on their relative ‘rights and obligations’ scape A joint operation Interest Js accounted for between assets, llabilities, revenue artd
expense, whilst a joint venture is resincted to equity accounting

ft becomes effective for accounting penods beginning on or after 1 January 2013 it has been adopted for use within the ELI for aecounting penods beginning on
or after 1 January 2014 The mpact to the Group of IFRS 11 Is likely to be Immaterial

+ IFRS 12 (Disclosure of interests m Other Entities (2011))
This new standard applies to entities that have an Interest in a subsidiary, a joint arrangement, an associate or an unconsolidated structured entity Itaims to
provide disclosure transparency on the reporting entity's nsks associated with its interests in other entites, in conjunction with IFRS 10's application which
determines the exstence of such entities

Itbecomes effective for accounting penods beginning on or after + January 2013 It has been adopted for use within the EU for accounting pertods beginning on
or after + January 2014 The mpact to the Group of the additional disclosure requirements 1s likely to be mmatenal

© IFRS 13 (Fair Value Measurement (2011)}
This new standard defines fair value and also acts as tts single authonty, dealing primarily with both its measurement and disclosure It does not require far
value measurements in addition to those alreacly required, or permitted by other IFRSs, and 1s not intended to establish valuation standards or affect valuation
practices outside of financeal reporting

The Group will adopt the standard for the accounting penod beginning on 1 January 2013 With the exception of additional disclosure, the impact to the Group
Of (FRS 13 Is likely to be immaterial

* Amended IAS 1 (Presentation of Items of Other Comprehensive Income)
This amendment proposes to split the presentation of other comprehensive income into two sections The different sectons wil distinguish between items
which may or may not be recycled into any future income or expense

+ Revised IAS 19 (Employee Benefits (2011))
‘This revision covers the standard’s recognition, measurement and presentabon criteria with regard to a pension scheme’s expense contained within IAS 19's
‘defined benefit’ scope Actuanat gams or losses will now be fully recognised within income or expense, thus removing ihe previous partial recognition of
‘comdor and spreading’

Furthermore, the revision eflminates the ‘expected retum on assets’ from the measurement of 2 pension’s expense and drects ertes to metead charge a cost
of finance against its net unfunded habiity positon

When determing a discount rate that calculates a pension obligaon's present value, reference should be made to market yelds These yields should be
matched fp high quality corporate bonds, in terms of both the obligation's currency and matunty, ahead of any previously assumed government bond yeld
reference point.

The Group will adopt the standard for the accounting penod beginning on 1 January 2013 The impact of the revision tolAS 19 is {ikely to be material to the
Group, due to the tkely impact on the pension costs recharged to the Group

Amended IAS 27 (Consolidated and Separate Financiat Statements (2011)
This amendment occurs as a direct consequence of IFRS 10's issuance, which now acts as the new single autharty on consolidabon requrements AS 27's
scope has therefore reduced and focuses more specifically on separate financial statements’ accounting bases. An entity shail therefore prepare its separate
financial statements using one of two bases, ether at cost or m accordance with IFRS 9

(t becomes effective for accounting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2013 Ithas been adopted for use within the EU for accountng periods beginning on
or after 1 January 2014 The impact of the amendment to IAS 27 ts likely to be smmatenal to the Group

© Amended IAS 28 (Investments in Associates and Jont Ventures (2011)
This amendment occurs as a direct consequence of IFRS 11's issuance, which now acts as the new single authority on jont arrangements [AS 28 changes in
order to consistently apply equity accounting methods for both joint ventures and associates. An entity shail therefore use !AS 28's equity accounting method,
when accounting for any of IFRS 11's joint arrangements
lt becomes effective for accounting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2013 It has been adopted for use within the EU for accounting periods beginning on
or after 1 January 2014 The impact of the amendment to IAS 28 1s likely to be immaterial to the Group

© Amendments to IFRS 7 (Disclosures Offsetting Financial Assets and Liabilties )

Amendments to lAS 32 (Offsetting Financial Assets and Financzal Liabittes )
The current offsetting model in IAS 32 requires an entity to offset a financial asset and financial lability only when the entty currently has a legally enforceable
fight of set-off and mtends either to settle on a net basis or to realise the asset and setife the lability simultaneously This amendment clanfies the position when
offsetting financial assets and financial fabilties The legal nght of set-off must be available today and legally enforceable for all counterparties in the normal
course of business, as well as in the event of default, msolvency or bankruptcy The amended disclosures will require more extensive disclosures than are
currently required The disclosures focus on quantitatve information about recognised financial mstruments that are offsetin the statement of financial positon,
as well as those recognised financial instruments that are subject to master netting or similar arrangements srespectve of whether they are offset.

Itbecomes effective for accounting periods beginning on or after 1 January 2014 thas been adopted for use within the EU for accounting penods beginning on
or after 1 January 2014 The impact of the amendment to IAS 32 1s likely to be immaterial to the Group

Other standards and interpretations have been issued but these are not considered to be relevant to the Group's operations

3) Revenue recognition

a) interest income and expense

Interest come and expense is recognised on an effectrve interest rate (EIR) basis, inclusive of directly attnbutable mcremental transaction costs and fees including
arrangement and broker fees, valuation and solicitor costs, discounts and premiums where appropriate and early redemption fees

The EIR basis spreads the interest income and expense over the expected life of each instrument. The EIR! the rate that, at the inception of the instrument,
exactly discounts expected future cash payments and receipts through the expected life of the instrument back to the initial carrying amount. When calculating the
EIR, the Group estmates cash flows considerng all contractual terms of the instrument (for example, prepayment options) but does not consider assets’ future
credit losses except for assets acquired at a deep discount.

For assets acquired at a value significantly below the carrying value in the acquiree’s financial statements because they have incurred loss, expectations of future
loss are higher than at originabon, and interest spreads have widened because of detenorating market conditions, the calculation of EIR is the same as shown
above with the exception that the estimates of future cash flows include credit losses

16) Cash and cash equivaients
Cash and cash equivalents comprises cash balances and balances with a maturity of three months or less from the acquisiton date, which are readily convertible

17) Income tax

a} Overmew

Tax on the income statement for the year compnses current and deferred tax, which ts recognised in the mcome statement except to the extent that it relates to
items recognised directly in equity, in which case it is recognised in the statement of comprehenstve income

b) Current tax
Current tax is the expected tax payable on the taxable income for the year, using tax rates enacted or substantively enacted at the balance sheet date, and any
adjustment to tax payable in respect of previous years

c) Deferred tax

Deferred tax is provided using the balance sheet method, providing for temporary differences between the carrying amounts of assets and liabilities for financial
reporting purposes and the amounts used for taxation purposes The amount of deferred tax provided for ts based on the expected manner of reaisaton or
settlement of the carrying amount of assets and liabilities, using tax rates enacted or substantively enacted at the balance sheet date

A deferred tax asset is recognised only to the extent that itis probable that future taxable profits wil be available against which the asset can be utilised
Deferred tax assets are renewed at each reporting date and are reduced to the extent that it ss no longer probable that the related tax benefit will be reaksed

18) Pension costs

a) Defined contribution basis

With effect from 6 April 2006, the Group, along with other businesses within The Co-operative Group, has participated in the Co-operative Group Pension
{average career earnings) Scheme (the Pace scheme, the assets of which are held in a separate fund admmistered by trustees The Group's de facto participation
level1s on a defined contribution basis, as it pays frxed sums into the fund on a regular basis This level of participation is required because the Pace defined
benefit scheme exposes the participating businesses to actuanal risks associated with the current and former employees of other group companies, with the result
that there is no consistent and refiable basis for allocating Pace's Kabilities, assets and costs to individual companies participating in the scheme Therefore pension
costs in respect of the scheme are accounted for on a defined contribution basis and recognised as an expense m the mcome statement as incurred

b) Former Britannia Building Society pension scheme

Following the transfer of engagements of Britannia Building Society, the Britannia defined benefit pension scheme transferred to the Co-operative Financial
Services Management Services Limited (CFSIMS), a fellow subsidiary undertaking of The Co-operative Banking Group In accordance with policy 18 a, the pension
costs in respect of this scheme are accounted for, by the Bank, ona defined contribution basis and recognsed as an expense in the nncome stalement as incurred

denommnated in foreign currencies that are stated at far values are translated to sterfing at the exchange rales prevailing at the dates the values were determmed

Roles and Responsibilities

The Banking Group operates a three tines of defence governance model to ensure appropnate responsibilty and accountability 1s allocated to the management,
reporting and escalation of risks .

Busmess management act as the first ine of defence They are responsible for lentfying where the business area is exposed to nsks, including from the
development of new products, processes or other business change They also manage the nsks that reside within their business areas on a day to day basis,
implementing effective monitonng and control processes to ensure that the business’ nsk profile is understood and maintained within the Board defined nsk
appetite

The risk function act as the second line of defence They oversee and challenge the implementation and monitoring of the risk framework and consider current and
emerging asks across the Bank They also provide review and challenge of the delegated authonty framework and oversee appropnate escalation of breaches,
tnitigating actions and reporting to the ERC

Internal Audit act as the third line of defence They are responsible for independently venfying that the princpal risk control framework has been Implemented as
Intended across the business and Independently challenge the overall management of the framework to provide assurance to the Audit Committee and senior
management on the adequacy of both the first and second lines

Recovery and Resolution Planning (RRP)
RRP Is a Bank of England/FSA requirement, which has two distinct elements

the recovery pian is the Group's menu of options for addressing a range of financial stresses caused by dhosyncratic problems, market wide stresses,
or both, and
© resolution planning is the provision of information and analysis to the authontes, in order to help them prepare a resolution plan for the Group

There are six separate modules for RRP, alt of which have gone through formal governance approval The approval route ts as follows

© Executive team,
© ERC,

* BRC, and

* the Board

In addition, as the recovery plan (module two) has been live since June 2012, a series of management information triggers and early waming signals are reported
each month to the ERC, for the committee to determine whether to recommend the snvocation of the recovery plan

Impairment

Alan is considered to be Individually impaired when
arrears outstanding are equivalent to 30 days or more, or

© the loan 1s more than 90 days past its due date for final repayment but has not been repaid, or

* the account is in litigation or the property is in possession, or

© the estimated forced sale value of the collateral on a roll up mortgage has reduced below the loan balance outstanding
Loans meeting any of these criteria are considered impaired even if a loss is not expected in the event of repossession

Loans are also assessed for impairment where any of the folowing cnterla apply, although thase factors alone are not deemed to be objective avdence of
impairment and therefore a collective provision is held against potential losses

© the joan is in arrears by less than 30 days, or

© there has been a matertal deterioration in the borrower's extemal credit score in the last 12 months, or
© the Ioan.s denttfied as beng subject to forbearance (refer to forbearance section)

On the Optimum portfotfo onty, any loans not meeting any of the preceding cntena are assessed for Impamment based on the probability that they will move

to 90 days in arrears within the next three months

Forbearance
Anumiber of options are avaiable to borrowers in financial difficully Repossession of the property wil only be considered when all other avenues have been
explored The prease treatment selected depends on the borrower's individual arcumstances, but may include

* arrangements to repay outstanding arrears over a period of time, by making payments above the contractual amount The loan is deemed to be mpared, until
the arrears have been Cleared in full

* short term concessions, where the borrower Is allowed to make reduced repayments (or, in exceptional dcumstances, no repayments} on a temporary basis
fo assist with a short term financial hardshi> In these cases the shortfall on the repayments will accrue as arrears and the loan is deemed to be imparred,

payment holdays are allowed as part of a customer's flexible mortgage contract. Payment holidays are not knowingly allowed for customers expanencing
financial hardship However, in the absence of evidence to the contrary, loans that have had @ payment holiday in the last wo years are treated as being subject
to forbearance,

temporary conversion of a mortgage to interest only repayments The capital repayments due m this penod are spread over the remaining term on conversion
back to capital repayment as part of an instalment recalculation exercsse;

© apermanent switch from capital and mterest repayments to interestonty repayments, provided the borrower declares they have an altemative means of
repaying the capital at the end of the term,

* an extension io the mortgage term to reduce the borrower's repayments Loans that have had a term extension in the last two years (or since 2008 in the case
of the Optimum portfolio) are considered tp be subject to forbearance,

* capitalisation, where outstanding arrears are atided to the capital value of the loan to be repaid over the remaining term This is only considered where itis
appropnate for the borrower's crcumstances, and where the borrawer has been performing for atleast sxmaonths Dunng 2012, 20 (2011 90) tans had
arrears capitalised of £0 03m (2011 £0 3m) These foans are not treated as impaired unless the borrower meets another impairment trigger (eg misses a
mortgage paymeng, and

© where applicable we will work with the customer as part of a government support scheme Unmet loan payments will continue to accrue as arrears untill such
time as the loan is cleared, or the outstanding payments are brought up to date

Forbearance

A number of forbearance options are available to borrowers in financial difficulty These include arrangements to repay arrears over a period of tme or to accept
payments of less than the contractual amount during a period of temporary financial hardship In these cicumstances, the loan continues to be reported as

© reducing overdrafts this option is used to reduce the overdraft levels on current accounts in excess of the agreed formal overdraft or \imit reduction applied by
the Group For this situation, the customer Is given up to eight months to move the account back within the agreed Emit;

© re-ages there are a small number of credit card re-ages performed each month for customer service reasons, and

debt consolidation Joans in certain circumstances, customers are offered a consofidaton ioan to pay off other intemal debt.

within corporate compnses corporate banking, business banking and business sermices. Non-core business ines include activites not congruent with the current

from mcome generation Lending discretions are based on the nsk profile of the customer and the amount of exposure The lending discretions of the Credit Risk

stability of the counterparty and its ability to withstand such change

removed from the watchlist

this will contnue

The Group also reviews monthly all higher risk loans (Including those accounts subject to forbearance} and considers the potential loss which might artse were the
borrowers to fail (notwithstanding that continued trading remains the expectation)

Individual impairment provisions are raised at the point when business performance is assessed to have detenorated to the extent that there ts a real risk of loss of
principal, interest or fees Provisions wil be required on some or all of the entire shortfall between the securlty hefd and the loan balance outstanding and represent
areakstic assessment of the likely net loss after realisation of any security

Objective evidence of impairment may include but is not limited to

® aninstalment on a loan account beng overdue, or having been m excess of its mut (or being overdrawn without an agreed Emit) for 90 days or more;

© an event fikely to result in Insolvency which may Invotve bankruptcy, or the appointment of an administrative receiver, iquidator or administrator; or

© ifthe Group considers that at some point (normally taken within the next 12 months) actions such as an issue of formal demand will be required in order
to achieve full repayment.

Forbearance

Ifthe Group is convinced of a customer's abtity and commitment to address therr financial difficulties, t may agree to grant concessions to the onginal contractual

terms. Such concessions typically include

© restructuring, warving or reserving rights m the event of covenant breaches,
© postponement of principal payments,

© restructures of principal payments,

* extension of loan matunites,

© partial or full capitalisation of interest payments, or

© swap restructures

{n additon to the above, other forbearance concessions the Group may grant are considered to be olyective endence of impairment and include

a partial wnite off of debt, following which the account continues to be classified as impaired for at least six months, or
* amatenal postponement or forgiveness of interest or ‘soft’ rates or walver or reduction of normal fees and charges, the accounts must remain impalred whle

such favourable terms are being applied

1. Credit risk contnued

Treasury operates a risk based approach which monttors counterparty mits and exposure via a credit risk register The counterparties or the assets held are
Monitored against a Board approved matnx of nsk tolerance and associated indicators The credit risk regster is updated for rating actions, market events and
financial results as they are announced which may influence a change in risk status and possible escalaton requirng management actons and inclusion on
the watchlist

The Treasury nsk team remews the entire portfolio and watchlists monthly for appropnate risk status bandings and any associated management actions.

As at 31 December 2012 there were no high nsk watchist exposures (2011 £277m) All high nsk watchis! exposures, consising entirely of debt secunty
holdings, reached final maturity durmg the period to 31 December 2012 and were repaid m full

15 2 Indirect exposures

Treasury nsk management menitoning extends beyond the direct nsk incurred through counterparty trading, to the underlying exposures (eg to penpheral eurozone
countries) which Treasury's counterparties may maintain on their own balance sheets In analysing each counterparty's secondary exposure we assess the
vulnerability and impact on that firm should it suffer different degrees of losses

Where secondary sovereign exposure or contagion risk is deemed to undermune the performance of the counterparty, remedial management actions are taken in
Tespect of Treasury's counterparty limits and exposure, often well ahead of any associated rating actions

2. Liquidity risk
2.1 Capital and liquidity framework
The Group's capital and Fquidity nsk management framework compnses

© a defined risk appebte, controls and governance in the Group's capital and kiquidity management policies,

articulation of how capital and liquidity nsk 6 wentified, measured, monstored and managed in the intemal Capslal Adequacy Assessment Process (ICAAP),
Individual Liquidity Adequacy Assessment (ILAA) and procedures and governance In place to mitigate the risk,

© capital and fiquidity nsk quantification and migaton techniques and processes,

* management actons linked through to stress testing and capital and fiquidity planning models, enabling a method of mitigating the effects of a number of stress
scenarios for varying penods of time and to ensure that the Group operates within its agreed nsk appette parameters in all planning models,

© ongoing development and enhancement of the Group's capital and liquidity nsk appetite framework, and

© a process to attribute the cost, benefit and nsks of liquidity to specific busiess Ines via the Group's Funds Transfar Pricing mechanism

2.2 Liquidity risk
The Board's nsk appetite for liquidity risk is defined sn terms of

» surwval penods which measure the degree of sufficiency of liquid assets to support the Group's actmity over time under a number of stress scenarios,
adherence to strategic liquidity risk measures, and
compliance with all regulatory liquidity nsk limits.

The stress tests encompass survival across various timescales (from three months to one year} and a range of adverse liquidity events, both firm specific and
market wide, which cover al aspects of the liquidity nsk to which the Group ts exposed These stress tests nclude a number of downgrade scenanos, from one
notch to mutt-notch long term and short tarm downgrades

The strategic measures approved by the Board include

© customer loan/deposit ratio, 92% (2011 94%) — the ratio of customer loans to customer deposits,
encumbrance ratio, 27% (2011 24%) — the rabo of encumbered assets drded by total assets,

© regulatory mits — intemal Liquidity Guidance and Net Stable Funding ratio, and

© internal liquidity stress tests — the survival penod of the Group under a range of stressed scenanos

23 Liquidity risk management overview

Liquidity risk arising from the structure of the balance sheet (structural liquidity) is managed In line with poles developed byLMC and ALCO The Group's liquidity
management policies are reviewed and approved annually by the BRC (on behalf of the Board) and compliance reviewed by LMC and monthly by ALCO

The Group's policy is to ensure that sufficient funds are avallable at all times to meet demands trom depostors, to fund agreed aclvances, to meet other
commitments as and when they fall due, and to ensure the Board risk appetite is met.

The Group monitors its liquidity position on a dally basis and has a weekly LMC which operates to oversee the operational Uquidity management A range of
indicators to detect early signs of fiquaity nsk either in the market or specific to the Group are atso monitored. LMC discuss the actual iquidity positon and
projected postion incorporating business plans More frequent meetings are held if necessary, 1e when the markets have a heightened period of stress or liquidity
shortage. The meatings ensure that the business plans are accurate and can be flexed as required

The liquidity position fs reported monthly to ALCO and the Board Italso monitors the adequacy of Its contro’s to provide assurance that Uquidlty risk is being

appropnately managed, and regularly assesses its funding poston This is supported with detailed contingency funding ptans and recovery options which are
tested and reviewed on a requaar basis The Group's liquidity management framework fs designed in line with FSA BIPRU regulations and industry guidetines

Contingent funding plans
The Group uses any combination of these asset pools to manage tiquidity, with LAB and non-buffer assets used predomunantly for short term cashflow

Trovernents, with the own retained asset pool creating longer term or contingent liquidity Regutar realisation through repo transactions and outright sales

provide assurance that these asset pools remain sufficiently liquid.

3. Market risk

Market risk ts the risk of loss as a sesutt of the value of financial assets or labimes (including off-balance sheet instruments) being adversely affected by
movements in market rates or prices. This loss can he reflected in the near term earnings by changing net nterest income, or in the longer term because of
changes in the economic vahie of future cash flows.

The mam source of market nsk within the Group is dnven by msmaiches between the repncmg profiles of asset and fabiity customer products within the retall
and corporate businesses and certan charactensbes embedded within these products and basis nsk Treasury also create market risk through Its various portfolio
management and trading activites along with currency risk.

3.1 Interest rate risk

Interest rate risk policy statements, approved by the ERC on behalf of the Board, specify the scope of the Group’s wholesale market actMity, market risk fimits and
delegated authorities The policy is managed by the TMAC and ALCO Their prime task is to assess the interest rate risk inherent in the matunty and repnang
characteristics of the Group's assets and llabllites The Group seeks to minimise the volatiity of future earings from Interest rate changes and af interest rate risk
exposure is removed from the retail and CABB divsions and consolidated at the centre where it is managed trom the core balance sheet within agreed limits
Treasury is responsible for interest rate nsk management for the Group The principal analytical techniques nvolve assessing the impact of different Interest rate
scenarios and changes in balances over various time periods.

The Board recelves reports on the management of balance sheet nsk and TMAC and ALCO review the balance sheet nsk positions and the utilisation of wholesale
market risk limits

311 Non-treasury interest rate nsk
The Group (excluding wholesale) uses a gap report and earnings approach for managing interest rate nsk, focusing in detail on the sensitwty of assumed changes
ininterest rates on net interest come for one year Higher level analysis is performed for subsequent years

TMRC monitors the non-trading Interest rate risk which Is split between certain wholesale portfolios, banking and investment books, and the rest of the Group's
balance sheet. The following descnes the Group non-trading portfolios excluding these certam wholesale portfolios These positions are managed by Treasury
All interest rate nsk is centralised ito Treasury using appropriate transfer pnicing rates

Gap reports are based on defined tme penods ALCO set guidance mits around the gap, principally that the sum of positons maturing in greater than 12 months
and non-sensitive balances (includes non-matunty deposits) is no more than a set limit.

Non-matunty deposits which are non-mnterest beanng are separated into a stable ‘core’ element, based on a long run average, and the residual balance, which
can fluctuate In the gap report, the residual balance (along with interest bearing non-matunty deposits) are deemed to re-price or mature within one month
The ‘core’ non-matunty deposits are withn the non-sensitve balance on the gap report, along with non-dated capital and other non-sensitive balances

ALCO sets guidance around the treatment of non-sensrtive balances to remvest in fixed rate assets m penods up to five years to smooth the income based
upon the prevailing interest rate environment

Fisk mits are formally calculated at each month end. Interest rate nsk and effectiveness of hedging is monitored daily using gap positions, Incorporating new
business requirements. Draw down risk, in particular for fixed rate mortgages, is managed through weekly balance sheet meetings The asset and babdity
management team undertake hedges for interest rate nsk using dettvative Insttuments and investment securities which are executed via the Treasury markets
team to external wholesate markets, and loans and deposits which are executed internally with the Treasury markets team

Basis nsk ts the nsk that different assets and Eabilites repnce with reference to different mdices and at different umes. This exposes the Group to income volatility
if indices do not move na rato of one to one The overalf exposure to basis nsk has remamed a net base rate asset throughout 2012 as customers continue to
favour vanable rate mortgages (where the introductory rate is knked to Bank of England base rate} and administered and {med rate savings Basis skis monitored
by TMRC and ALCO monthly and action is taken as required, which includes pricing, new products or extemal hedging

The table ilustrates the greater than 12 month net gap positon at the end of the penod on the Group's balances, excuding wholesale treasury and customer
currency balances which are managed within the treasury nsk framework. The gap s driven by product pricing and product mix The gap Is calculated by placing
all assets and habiibes at the earhest of their repncing or matunty date and then summung by time band The aims to have assets evenly spread so that the Group
ts not exposed to sudden rate movements The net positon shows the amount that the Group is either over or under invested in the month A£100m positive gap
position would equate to the Group's Income increasing by £m per annum if rates increased by 19% The maximum sensitivity for the perlod shown below equates

312 Treasury interest rate nsk

Treasury executes short term funding and hedging transactons with the wholesale markets on behalf of the Group and tts customers Italso generates

and stop loss linuts

3121 Value at Risk (VaR)

VaR measures the dafy maximum potential gain or loss due to market volatility within a statstical confidence level of 95% and a one day holding pentod The VaR
Methodology empioyed fs historical simulation using a time series of one year to latest day and was £0 4m at 31 December 2012 for the trading portfolios (2011
£0 4m) The VaR methodology has inherent imitations in that market volatility in the past may not be a relable predictor of the future, and may not reflect the time
required to hedge or dispose of the position, hence VaR Is not used as the sole measure of risk.

3122 PV100

This iiustrates the change in valuation on a fixed income portfolo expenenced given a 1% increase and decrease in mterestrates, representing the treasury
banking book and treasury trading book. PV100 Is the effect on the net present value (NPV) of the wholesale portfolio to a parallel shift of 100 basis points upon
the base yield curve The effects of a 1% Increase in interest rates are (£3 Om) (2011 £15 1m) and a 1% decrease £14 6m (2011 (£16 Om)

4, Operationat risk
Operational nsk is defined within the Banking Group as the risk of loss resulting from inadequate or failed intemal processes, people and systems or extemal
events This encompasses the effectivenass of risk management techruques and controls to minimise these losses

In order to meet the Banking Group's appetite for operational nsk, appropriate controls are in place to mmumise the chance of any significant drstuption and to
protect our reputabon

Operational nsks are identified, managed and mitigated through ongoing nsk management practices mcluding nsk assessments, formal control procedures and
Contingency planning. Operational nsks and key controls are forrnally reviewed on a regular basis Significant operational risks and the assocsated capital
requirements are regularly reported fo the ORC, the ERC and the BRC: These meet regularly to monitor the suitability of the nsk management framework and
management of significant nsks within the Banking Group

Business continuity arrangements are in place which set out to mmimise the risk of disruption in the event of a sudden, unplanned occurrence that coutd seriously
disrupt business operabons This motudes developing and exercising crisis and incident management teams to maintain appropriate preparectness in the event of a
major operational disruption

The Banking Group also has a corporate insurance programme to transfer specific nsks to msurers as past of rts nsk management approach

Reputationa! nsk is defined as the nsk assocated with an issue which could in some way be damaging to the brand of The Co-operatve Banking Group either
through rts strategic decisions, business performance, an operabonal failure or external perception

As part of the assessment and control of this risk, business performance and nsk profile across all risk themes are closely monitored and reviewed The busness
proactively monitors and manages media, public and customer opinion and works closely with extemal rating agenctes to ensure fair and balanced representation
This approach helps manta member, customer and market confidence

6. Strategic and business risk

Stratagic and business risk arises from changes to the Banking Group’s business and the enwronment in which it operates, specifically the risk of not being able to
Carry out the Banking Group's business plan and desired strategy

The Group's strategy 1s to grow our business in order to achieve the Banking Group's vision of becommg the compelling, co-operative altematve in the markets mn
which we compete

The main nsks to delivering the business plan and achieving the strategy are the challenging economic environment, the changing regulatory environment and the
significant change programme being managed within the Group and the wider Co-operative Group

The Board and Executive set and monitor the strategic plan in the light of this background having consxdered the stresses that extreme but plausible scenanos
could have upon it.

7. Conduct risk

Conduct risk fs the risk that the Banking Group's behaviours, offerings or interactons wil result in unfair outcomes for customers Accordingly, conduct nsk may
anse from any aspect of the way 4 business is conducted, the sole tast being whether the outcome is an unfair one for customers

The Banking Group rabgates and prevents emerging conduct nsk through established systems and controls including ongoing oversight and morutonng from risk
functions

‘Our submissions to the FSA in the penod have shown that the Bank and its individually regulated operations have complied mith all externally imposed capital
requirements

© Retail — customer focused products and services for individuals, sole traders and small businesses This includes mortgages, credit cards, consumer loans,

* CABB — customer focused products and services for busmesses, mcluding large corporate and commercial entities Itincludes loans, asset finance, current

of business are targeted for run down or exit CABB also includes the Optmum closed beok of acquired mortgage portfofios and mortgages orignated wa

17. Derivative financial instruments

The Bank, as principal, has entered into vanous denvatrves either as a trading actmity, which includes propdetary transactions and customer facilitation, or as a
hedging actluity for the management of interest rate risk, equity risk and foreign exchange rate risk. Poslive and negative far values have not been netted as the

Group does not have a legal nght of offset

Derivatives held for trading purposes

Trading transactions are wholly interest rate related contracts including swaps, caps and floors, forward rate agreements and exchange traded futures. Trading
transactions include derivatives where the Bank enters into a transaction to accommodate a customer together with the corresponding hedge transaction

Non-trading derivatives

Non-trading transactions comprise denvatives held for hedging purposes to manage the asset and hability posibons of the Group Derwvatives used to manage
interest rate related positions include swaps, caps and floors, forward rate agreements and exchange traded futures The foreign exchange rate positions are
managed using forward currency transactions and swaps Equity risk is managed using equity swaps.

During the year the Bank has entered into fair value hedges to mitgate price movements due to interest rate sensitivities

Financial Services Compensation Scheme (FSCS) levies

The FSCS has provided compensaton to customers of financial nsttutons following the collapse of deposit takers m 2008 The compensation paid out to
consumers 1S currently funded through loans from HM Treasury The Group wall be lable to pay @ proportion of the outstanding borrowings that the FSCS has
borrowed from HM Treasury Additionally the Group Is obliged to pay Its share of management expenses and compensation based upon the Group's proportion of
the total market protected deposits at 31 December of each year

The ulimate FSCS levy to the industry as a result of the 2008 collapses cannot currently be estimated reliably as it is dependent on other factors that may affect
amounts payable and the trnng of amounts payable, including changes in interest rates, potental recovenes of assets by the FSCS and the level of protected
deposits

The Group has provided £38 6m (2011 £25 Om) for its share of the levies ralsed by the FSCS including the interest on the toan from HM Treasury in respect of the
levy years to 31 March 2014 The prowsion includes £27 7m in respect of the interest levy (2011 £25 Om) The Group's interest levy provision caicutaton includes
estimates of the total FSCS levy in each levy year and estimates of the Group's market participation in each levy year During 2012, the FSCS indicated that It
expected to raise a capital levy to cover an estimated shortfall in excess of £800m in the amounts recovered from the falled banks to repay HM Treasury loans
made to the FSCS The Group's share of such a levy is estimated at £24 Om and the Group has provided £10 9m in the current year m respect of this

deferred tax asset at 31 December 2012 has been calculated based on the rate of 23% substantvely enacted at the balance sheet date Ithas not yet been
Possible to quantify the full antapated effect of the announced further 2% rate reduction, although this wil further reduce the Group's future current tax charge
and reduce the Group's deferred tax asset accordingly

34, Pensions

Defined contribution basis

The Group parbcipates in The Co-operative Group Pengion (average career earings) Scheme (Pace) Pace is a defined benefit scheme, the assets of which are
held In a separate fund administered by tustees As a group wide pension scheme, Pace exposes the participating businesses to actuarlal nsks associated with
the current and former employees of other Group companies, with the result that there is no consistent and relabble basss for allocating hablites, assets and costs
to individual compantes participating in the scheme Therefore, pension costs In respect of the scheme are accounted for on a defined contribution basis and
recogmsed as an expense in the mcome statement as incurred, based on a fixed percentage as agreed wth the trustees.

a, Use of financtal instruments
The use of financial nstrumens is essential ta the Bank's business activites, and financial nstruments constitute a significant proportion of the Bank's assets
and babitties The man financial instruments used by the Group, and the purposes for which they are held, are outlined below

Loans and advances to customers and customer accounts

The provision of banking faciities to customers 1s the pnme activity of the Bank, and loans and advances to customers and customer accounts are major
constituents of the balance sheet. Loans and advances to customers include retail mortgages, corporate loans, credit cards, unsecured retall lending and
overdrafts Customer accounts include retail and corporate current and savings accounts The Bank has detailed policies and procedures to manage risks.
Retail mortgage lending and much of the lending to corporate and business banking customers is secured

Loans and advanices to banks and investment securities.
Loans and advances to banks and investment securities underpin the Bank's tiquidity requirements and generate tncremental net Interest and trading Income
Held for trading vestments are traded solely for short term profit.

Deposits by banks and debt securthes in tssue

The Bank issues medium term notes withm an established euro medium term note programme and also issues certificates of deposit and commercial paper as
part of its normal treasury activites These sources of funds, alongside other borrowed funds, are invested in marketable investment grade debt securities and
short term wholesale market placements and are used to fund customer foans

‘Other borrowed funds
The Bank utilises a broad spread of capital funds in adaition to ordinary share capita! and retained eamings, when appropriate, the Bank issues preference shares
and perpetual and fed term subordinated notes

Tharket and credit nsk control procedures as are applied to other wholesale market instruments and are aggregated with other exposures to monitor total
counterparty exposure which is managed within approved limits for each counterparty

The carrying values of financial instruments measured at fair value are determined in comptance with the accounting policies on pages 40 to 50 and according to
the following hierarchy

Level 1 - Quoted market prices in active markets
Financial instruments with quoted prices for identical instruments in active markets The best evidence of fair vatue is a quoted market price in an actively traded
market.

Level 2 ~ Valuation techniques usmg observable mputs
Financeal instruments with quoted pnces for sumitar mstruments in active markets or quoted pnces for identical or similar instruments mn Inactive markets and
financal mstruments valued using models where af significant inputs are observable.

The valuation techniques used to value these instruments employ only observable market data and relate to the following assets and Ilablities

Loans and advances to customers
Loans and advances to customers include corporate loans of £142 4m (2011 £101 7m) which are fair valued through income or expense using observable inputs
Loans helt at fair value are valued at the sum of all future expected cash flows, discounted using a yield curve based on observable market inputs

Investment securities — available for sale
Farr value is based on available market prices Where this information ts not available, far value has been estimated using quoted market pnces for secunties with
similar credit, matunty and yteld characteristics

Derivative financial Instruments

OTC (le non-exchange traded) dertvatives are valued using valuation models which are based on observable market data Valuation models calculate the present
value of expected future cash flows, based upon ‘no arbitrage’ pnncples The Bank enters into vanilla foreign exchange and interest rate swap denvatnves, for
which modelling techniques are standard across the industry Examples of inputs that are generally observable include foreign exchange spot and forward rates,
and benchmark interest rate curves

Customer accounts — capital bonds
The estimated fair value of customer accounts — capita! bonds Is based on mdependent third party valuations using forecast future movements in the
appropriate indices

Level 3— Valuation techniques using unobservable mputs
This 1s used for financial instruments valued using models where one or more significant inputs are not observable

The small proportion of financial assets valued based on significant unobservable inputs are analysed as folows

Loans and advances to customers

Loans and advances to customers include 26 year fixed rate mortgages of £11 2m (2011 £12 4m) whichare fair valued through Income or expense usIAg
unobservable mputs 25 year txed rate mortgages are valued using future interest cash flows at the fixed customer rate and estimated schedule of customer
tepayrnents. Cash flaws are discounted at a credit adjusted discount rate, the credit adjustment \s based on the average margin of new long dated (five years or
greater) fixed rate business written in the last sx months, and sutyect to quarterly review The eventual timng of future cash flows may be different from that
forecast due to unpredictable customer behaviour, particularly on a 25 year product The valuation methedology takes account of credit risk and has increased the
valuation by £0 Sm in 2012 (2011 £0 5m decrease) A reasonable change in the assumptions would not result in any matenal change mn the valuation

Equity shares
Equity shares pnmmanty relate to investments held im Vocalnk Limited which are unquoted shares The valuation of these shares Is based! an the Group's percentage
shareholding and the net asset value of the company according ta its most recently published financial staternents

Arnounts owed to other Co-operative Group ut
Amounts owed to other Co-operatve Group undertalongs are to the Silk Road Finance Number One plc (Sik Rd 1) subsidiary, relating fo the legal transfer of loans
and advances on secuntisation The amounts are fair valued to eliminate an accounting mismatch of the swap denvaive as discussed above

Revaluabon of the £1,764 4m (2011 £2,130 6m) mortgage poo! from carrying to fawr value is based on assumed timing of future Mortgage capital and revenue
receipts, discounted to present value using a credit adjusted discount rate

Dunng 2012, the Bank's amounts awed to other Co-operaive Group undertakings decreased due to reanalysis of mtercompany balances

The amortisation profile is as per the swap’s valuation methodology, assuming some annual prepayment, buts extended beyand any bond matunty, unbt all the
mortgages themselves mature, which is circa 25 years Similarly, the revenue receipts are calculated as per the swap valuation methodology, but extended untl all
the mortgages mature For fmed rate mortgages, revenue receints are based on fixed customer rates withn the assumed amortisation profila For tracker, SVR
and discount products, revenue receipts are assumed to be based on forward LIBOR rates plus the product margins Fixed and tracker mortgages are assumed
to revert to SVR at the end of any offer perlod All mortgages in the covered bond pool were onginated pre 31 December 2007

The fair value of the swap is based on a valuation mode! that reflects the mortgage cash flaws over a three year penod using a discount rate based on LIBOR
spreads This denvatve eltminates on consoldation

Derivative financial struments
Denvatve financial instruments including intemal interest rate swaps have bean entered into between the Bank and Sik Rd +

The purpose of the swaps 1s to convert the fixed and base rate inked revenue receipts of the pool of mortgage assets to the same LIBOR tnked basis as the
intercompany loan Under this swap arrangement Sik Rd 1 pays to the swap counterparty, the monthly motgage revenue receipts of the pool of assets and
receives from the swap counterparty LIBOR plus a contractual spread on the same notional balance, the spread being sufficient to cover the intercompany loan
and any expenses

The swaps are valued based on an assumed amortisation profile af the pool of assets to the bond maturity date (assuming some annual prepayment), an assumed
profile of customer receipts over this period, arid LIBOR prediction using forward rates Swap cash flows are discounted to present value using mud-yeld curve
Zefo coupon rates, ie no adjustment is made for credit losses, nor for transaction or any other costs

The far value of the swaps is based on a valuabon model that reflects the mortgage cash flows over a three year pened using a discount rate based on LIBOR
spreads These denvatives elimmate on consolidation

match the maturity dates of the underlying assets.

mortgage portfofios exceeds the administration costs of these mortgages The Group continues to bear the credit nsk of these mortgage assets

Slotting The regulatory defined approach used for rating the risk level of Corporate lending using a broad scope of financial,
political and transactional factors The regulatory slotting categones are listed as follows

— Standardised’ businesses lack sufficient information upon which detaited credit analysis can be undertaken for
which models have yet to be developed,

~ ‘Strong’ businesses have little competition, high demang, significant govermental support and enforceable
catateral,

~ ‘Good’ businesses have Limited competition, stable demand, good governmental support and enforceable
collateral;

—~‘Satsfactory’ businesses have a broad cornpetton base with limited levels of demand, governmental support
and collateral,

~ ‘Weak’ businesses operate at a disadvantage to competition, display a decinng trend of demand, wath no
governmental support and no clear collateral, and

~ ‘Default’ businesses are considered unlikely to repay their credit obligations (see ‘Impalred loans’)
